=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an even integer n representing the number of houses arranged in a straight line, and a 2D array cost of size n x 3, where cost[i][j] represents the cost of painting house i with color j + 1.
The houses will look beautiful if they satisfy the following conditions:

No two adjacent houses are painted the same color.
Houses equidistant from the ends of the row are not painted the same color. For example, if n = 6, houses at positions (0, 5), (1, 4), and (2, 3) are considered equidistant.

Return the minimum cost to paint the houses such that they look beautiful.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 4, cost = [[3,5,7],[6,2,9],[4,8,1],[7,3,5]]
Output: 9
Explanation:
The optimal painting sequence is [1, 2, 3, 2] with corresponding costs [3, 2, 1, 3]. This satisfies the following conditions:

No adjacent houses have the same color.
Houses at positions 0 and 3 (equidistant from the ends) are not painted the same color (1 != 2).
Houses at positions 1 and 2 (equidistant from the ends) are not painted the same color (2 != 3).

The minimum cost to paint the houses so that they look beautiful is 3 + 2 + 1 + 3 = 9.

Example 2:

Input: n = 6, cost = [[2,4,6],[5,3,8],[7,1,9],[4,6,2],[3,5,7],[8,2,4]]
Output: 18
Explanation:
The optimal painting sequence is [1, 3, 2, 3, 1, 2] with corresponding costs [2, 8, 1, 2, 3, 2]. This satisfies the following conditions:

No adjacent houses have the same color.
Houses at positions 0 and 5 (equidistant from the ends) are not painted the same color (1 != 2).
Houses at positions 1 and 4 (equidistant from the ends) are not painted the same color (3 != 1).
Houses at positions 2 and 3 (equidistant from the ends) are not painted the same color (2 != 3).

The minimum cost to paint the houses so that they look beautiful is 2 + 8 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 2 = 18.

 
Constraints:

2 <= n <= 10^5
n is even.
cost.length == n
cost[i].length == 3
0 <= cost[i][j] <= 10^5

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's design code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, n: int, cost: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        INF = 10**30
        states = [(a,b) for a in range(3) for b in range(3) if a != b]
        # transitions[current_index] = list of next state indices valid
        trans = []
        for idx, (a,b) in enumerate(states):
            lst = []
            for j, (na,nb) in enumerate(states):
                if na != a and nb != b:
                    # states already have na != nb
                    lst.append(j)
            trans.append(lst)
        m = n // 2
        dp = [INF] * len(states)
        for idx, (a,b) in enumerate(states):
            dp[idx] = cost[0][a] + cost[n-1][b]
        for k in range(1, m):
            ndp = [INF] * len(states)
            left_cost = cost[k]
            right_cost = cost[n-1-k]
            for idx, val in enumerate(dp):
                if val >= INF: continue
                for j in trans[idx]:
                    na, nb = states[j]
                    nv = val + left_cost[na] + right_cost[nb]
                    if nv < ndp[j]:
                        ndp[j] = nv
            dp = ndp
        return min(dp)

This is clean. Need ensure `states` valid all a!=b. Transition condition na != a and nb != b. Since states includes na != nb, no need check. Good.

Could optimize by precomputing `next_states` as list of (j, na, nb) maybe:
trans = [[(j, states[j][0], states[j][1]) for j in ...]]

Now, let's maybe think if there is a way to further reduce code:
```
class Solution:
    def minCost(self, n: int, cost: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        INF = 10**30
        states = [(0,1),(0,2),(1,0),(1,2),(2,0),(2,1)]
        trans = []
        for a,b in states:
            trans.append([(j, na, nb) for j, (na,nb) in enumerate(states) if na != a and nb != b])
        m = n // 2
        dp = [cost[0][a] + cost[n-1][b] for a,b in states]
        for k in range(1,m):
            ndp = [INF]*6
            lc = cost[k]
            rc = cost[n-1-k]
            for i, cur in enumerate(dp):
                if cur == INF: continue # but cur can be INF? Use >= INF
                for j, na, nb in trans[i]:
                    val = cur + lc[na] + rc[nb]
                    if val < ndp[j]:
                        ndp[j] = val
            dp = ndp
        return min(dp)
```
Use `if cur >= INF`? Since INF large, no valid cost equals INF. Use `if cur == INF` okay but if some valid cost exactly INF? INF=1e30, max cost 1e10, so no. Use `if cur >= INF` robust. Good.

For i=0..n-2, positions i and i+1.
Left half i=0..m-2: positions i and i+1 => a_i and a_{i+1}.
Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... For i=n-2=2m-2, b_1 and b_0. So all b_k and b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2? Let's map: i=m corresponds b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}, so k=m-2? Actually b_{m-1} adjacent to b_{m-2}; that's pair k=m-2 transition? Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=0..n-2, positions i and i+1.
Left half i=0..m-2: positions i and i+1 => a_i and a_{i+1}.
Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... For i=n-2=2m-2, b_1 and b_0. So all b_k and b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2? Let's map: i=m corresponds b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}, so k=m-2? Actually b_{m-1} adjacent to b_{m-2}; that's pair k=m-2 transition? Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

For i=0..n-2, positions i and i+1.
Left half i=0..m-2: positions i and i+1 => a_i and a_{i+1}.
Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... For i=n-2=2m-2, b_1 and b_0. So all b_k and b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2? Let's map: i=m corresponds b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}, so k=m-2? Actually b_{m-1} adjacent to b_{m-2}; that's pair k=m-2 transition? Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=0..n-2, positions i and i+1.
Left half i=0..m-2: positions i and i+1 => a_i and a_{i+1}.
Right half i=m..n-2: positions i and i+1. Let i = n-1-k? Wait for i=m, pair k=m-1? Position m is b_{m-1}; position m+1 is b_{m-2}; so b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}. For i=m+1, b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; ... For i=n-2=2m-2, b_1 and b_0. So all b_k and b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2? Let's map: i=m corresponds b_{m-1} and b_{m-2}, so k=m-2? Actually b_{m-1} adjacent to b_{m-2}; that's pair k=m-2 transition? Wait b_{m-1} is inner pair k=m-1, b_{m-2} is pair k=m-2. Adjacent condition b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Our transition for k=m-2: b_{m-2} != b_{m-1}. Yes. For i=m+1: b_{m-2} and b_{m-3}; transition k=m-3. ... For i=2m-2: b_1 and b_0; transition k=0. So all transitions b_k != b_{k+1} for k=0..m-2. Good.
Middle adjacency i=m-1: positions m-1 (a_{m-1}) and m (b_{m-1}) => a_{m-1} != b_{m-1}. Pair constraint for k=m-1. Good.
Thus complete.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, n: int, cost: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        INF = 10**30

        # State: (color of left house, color of right house) in a symmetric pair.
        states = [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 0), (1, 2), (2, 0), (2, 1)]

        # Precompute valid transitions between consecutive symmetric pairs.
        transitions = []
        for a, b in states:
            nxt = []
            for j, (na, nb) in enumerate(states):
                if na != a and nb != b:
                    nxt.append((j, na, nb))
            transitions.append(nxt)

        m = n // 2

        # Initialize with the outermost pair: positions 0 and n-1.
        dp = [cost[0][a] + cost[n - 1][b] for a, b in states]

        # Process pairs from outside toward the center.
        for k in range(1, m):
            ndp = [INF] * 6
            left_cost = cost[k]
            right_cost = cost[n - 1 - k]

            for i, cur in enumerate(dp):
                if cur >= INF:
                    continue
                for j, na, nb in transitions[i]:
                    val = cur + left_cost[na] + right_cost[nb]
                    if val < ndp[j]:
                        ndp[j] = val

            dp = ndp

        return min(dp)
```
